In a BitNode past those (only yours) BN1's, which overestimates the growth, up to 5× in BN3 and BN11 (0.2), the unsafe side. The player's `hacking_money` and `hacking_grow` multipliers come from the medium snapshot, 1 where it doesn't say.
  - **Why 2:** see [the income suite](#the-income-suite).
- **The controller** (`DesiredStateController`, `src/desired-state.ts`; since #151 it also sends [work](#work); since #141 it also sends [intents](#intents): [the hacking programs](#the-hacking-programs), [home's RAM](#home-ram), [cloud servers](#cloud-servers), [the backdoors](#backdoors-for-the-hacker-factions), [the joins](#factions-and-their-work) and the owner's manual queue) keeps the control channel's desired state in step with the switches and the connected Agent's snapshots:
  - **Off** is sent at once, to each Agent as it connects too.
  - **On** waits for that Agent's own static and medium snapshots, which come in its first round, then sends the target (or none yet, while the servers get rooted) and the hosts. It never sends the last brain's or the last game's view:
    - a brain that restarts sends the running Agent the same state, which changes nothing;
    - an Agent after a reset is never given a target its game can't hack.
  - It keeps the target it last set, whether it earns, and the hosts preparing the next one, for the move to the next target (#184): across the Agent's connections while `lastAugReset` is unchanged. It forgets them on a reset or the switch off; a new brain starts without them.
  - The channel sends each connection a state once, then again on a change. It sends only to a welcomed Agent running the deployed code. While the brain holds, it sends only what starts nothing new: the hack loop off, or as last sent, and no intents or work ([protocol](../../docs/bridge/protocol.md#rules)).
  - The dashboard shows the controller's own view: on or off, waiting for the snapshots, the target and the number of hosts, and the target being prepared with its number of hosts, each with its hosts' hack threads added up (#187).
- **Wiring:** `main.ts` makes the controller before the control channel listens, with the save backups it asks before an irreversible intent, and the brain's log for the backdoors' lines (#195) and the factions' (#203); the faction rule's events go to the store through `recordEvents(desiredState, …)`, as the deployer's do. The simulator suite wires it the same way through `test/helpers/brain-in-simulation.ts` (`withDesiredState`). `brain-process.test.ts` doesn't cover it.
- **The suite** (`test/hack-loop-skeleton.test.ts`), in an 8 GB fresh start in the simulator:
  - The Agent roots the no-port servers and runs the loop on each against the brain's target (n00dles). The money grows by over $500,000 in 10 virtual minutes.
  - An owner's script already on foodnstuff is untouched, and the loop there has only the RAM it left.
  - Home never holds more than the Agent and one worker, the hack loop's included.
  - **A loop killed from outside** is back within one convergence (30 s) and a round.
  - **Off,** every loop is gone within two rounds and stays gone. On again, they're back.
  - **A brain restart** leaves the loops alone: the very same processes.
  - **After a disconnect,** once connected again, the Agent acts only on that brain's instructions. From a brain that sends none, a loop that stops stays stopped. (With no brain at all, it's [safe mode](#safe-mode).)
  - **With the desired state set directly:** only the named hosts run the loop, a new target restarts the loops against it, a dropped host's loop goes, and another deployment's loop is stopped.
  - **Each host its own target** (#184): five hosts earn on n00dles while foodnstuff and joesguns prepare joesguns. When all move to joesguns, only the five loops against n00dles are stopped and started, and the owner's script on foodnstuff is untouched. The Agent's log names both while preparing: "on, N threads (H hacking) on 5 servers, against n00dles; preparing joesguns on 2 servers (H hacking)".
  - **Each host's hack threads** (#187): a count over a host's loop threads is capped at them; a host the state doesn't name hacks with all its threads; a changed count, or a first count for a host that had none (as from an older safe-mode file), restarts that host's loop alone, and the others keep their pids; the Agent's log gives the threads hacking. With 13 threads hacking in all (1 to 6 a host), the hosts' hacks never take n00dles below 70% of its money once it's at 75%: `hack`'s `threads` option at work, in the game's own code. (All 37 threads would take about 15% a round, to about 64%: arithmetic, not something the test runs.)
  - **Home too full for the deploy worker:** home never holds more than the Agent, the owner's script and one worker, and the deploy worker runs within two rounds of there being room.
  - **A purchase the page can't make** (#136; the simulator's page has no sidebar): each try logs why, the next comes 30 s later, and home never holds more than the Agent and one worker. E2 buys on the real page ([packages/e2](../e2/README.md)).
  - **A home RAM upgrade already made** (#138; an upgrade from 4 GB on the fresh start's 8 GB): the worker reports it done from the game alone, without touching the page, so a repeat never buys a second doubling.

### The income suite

`test/hack-loop-income.test.ts` (#184, #187) starts from an 8 GB fresh start in BN1, with the brain's rules as on Steam but the batcher off (#206), so the hack loop is what's measured: the loop, the programs, home RAM and, since #194, cloud servers, all on. It runs until the loop leaves n00dles for a better target, within 3 virtual hours, then 45 virtual minutes more. It reproduced the Steam stall on `7990834`: the move to zer0 came at 45 to 48 virtual minutes on each of seeds 1 to 8, and money stayed flat for the 45 minutes after.

**What it checks** (in CI, seed 1, about 12 s):
- Money never goes more than 10 virtual minutes without rising (`INCOME_STALLED_AFTER_MS`): around the move (any stretch that overlaps the prepare's start to 10 minutes after the move), and over the whole run from money's first rise.
- The brain logs no income stall.
- The loop stays off n00dles.

Seed 1 prepares from 48 minutes, moves to max-hardware at 90, and goes 7.7 minutes at most without a rise: 2.3 minutes to spare.

**Other seeds:** set `HACK_LOOP_INCOME_SEEDS` to the seeds, `1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8`. In PowerShell that's `$env:HACK_LOOP_INCOME_SEEDS = "1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8"`; afterwards run `Remove-Item Env:HACK_LOOP_INCOME_SEEDS`, as it stays set for the session. Then run `npm test -- packages/brain/test/hack-loop-income.test.ts --silent=false`, which takes about 95 s. An empty entry, or one that isn't a whole number from 1, fails loudly. Each seed prints its prepare start, its move, its longest stretch around the move and over the run, its money at the end, and what it earned after the move (money's rises added up: the money at the end depends on when the last purchase came). Earned misses the income of any 10 s sample in which a purchase lands, since that sample's money falls.

**#187, the drain.** On `3d6e5a6` (#184), seeds 2 to 4 failed the whole run, after the move: once every host hacked max-hardware, the loops all saw 75% at once and hacked it together. Its money fell to almost nothing (seed 2: $124m to $111 at 96 minutes), and took over 20 minutes to grow back. With [each host's hack threads](#the-hack-loop), all 8 seeds pass:

| Seed | Longest without a rise, `3d6e5a6` → now (min) | Money at the end | Earned after the move |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 8.0 → 7.7 | $983m → $997m | $1.55b → $1.62b |
| 2 | **28.2** → 4.7 | $499m → $58m | $1.13b → $1.66b |
| 3 | **13.0** → 6.3 | $426m → $755m | $1.05b → $1.35b |
| 4 | **24.3** → 5.8 | $144m → $972m | $0.73b → $1.61b |
| 5 | 7.0 → 5.7 | $659m → $262m | $2.28b → $1.84b |
| 6 | 6.8 → 5.5 | $989m → $931m | $1.61b → $1.57b |
| 7 | 6.0 → 5.5 | $937m → $830m | $1.47b → $1.35b |
| 8 | 8.7 → 6.2 | $597m → $984m | $1.19b → $1.60b |
| All 8 | | $5.23b → $5.79b | $11.0b → $12.6b |
| 1 and 5–8 (never stalled) | | | $8.10b → $7.98b |

**Since #194,** the suite runs with cloud servers on, as on Steam, which the brain starts buying near the move: all 8 seeds pass, the longest stretch without a rise 8.5 minutes, and they earned $24.2b after the move, against the table's $12.6b. The table is #187's, before cloud servers.

**The income criterion** (#187, the owner's): over the 8 seeds, neither total, earned after the move nor money at the end, may fall. Both rose. The gain is the three seeds that stalled: on the five that never did, earned fell 1.5%, and seed 5's by 19.6% ($2.28b → $1.84b): the cost of capping hacks that didn't need it there.

- **`HACK_REGROW_ROUNDS`, tuned here:** at 0.5, nearly every hack capped, they earned about half; at 2, the table; at 3, the cap doesn't bind on max-hardware at the move, and seed 4 went 11.0 minutes without a rise.
- **Money at the end swings with purchase timing:** a purchase just before the end takes most of it (seed 2's $58m). In a trial with the rounds at 8, where it never binds on max-hardware, seeds 1 and 5 to 8 matched `3d6e5a6` to the dollar, while seeds 2 and 4, on a different path, ended lower ($474m and $77m) though they earned more ($1.93b and $1.63b).

## The hacking ceiling

"How good is our hacking" as a number (#196, M4 phase A slice A1): the most hacking income a second that RAM could earn at the player's hacking now, from the game's formulas, against what hacking actually earned. Two ceilings, both upper bounds:

- **The batcher ceiling, the score** the batcher (A7) is judged on: a batcher (HWGW) commits its grow and weaken threads before it knows whether the hack lands, so a failed hack wastes them.
- **The hacking ceiling,** reached only by a reactive hacker that grows and weakens just what a landed hack took. Nothing passes it.

**Per GB-second** (core's `batcherIncomePerGigabyteSecond` and `hackingIncomePerGigabyteSecond`, `packages/core/src/hacking-ceiling.ts`). On a target at its minimum security and maximum money M, where every figure is at its best, each dollar hacked costs at least:
- **hack:** 1/(c·p·M) threads of a 1.7 GB script for T, failed hacks included. c is the chance (`hackChance`, Hacking.ts:9-24), p one thread's share (`percentMoneyHackedPerThread`, Hacking.ts:44-57), and T the hack time (`hackTimeSeconds`, Hacking.ts:60-80).
- **grow:** 1/(M·k + 1) threads of a 1.75 GB script for 3.2T, k being one thread's growth log with the core bonus (`growthLogPerThread`, grow.ts:8-29; ServerHelpers.ts:287-290). Growing a share f back takes x threads with (M(1 − f) + x)·e^(kx) = M (grow.ts:38-58). With u = x/M − f, that is kx = −log(1 + u) ≥ −u, so x·(k + 1/M) ≥ f, with equality only as f → 0.
- **weaken:** removes what the hack adds (0.002 a successful thread) and what the grow adds (0.004 a thread) (Constants.ts:9; ServerHelpers.ts:209-211), at 0.05 × the core bonus a thread (Constants.ts:10), with a 1.75 GB script for 4T.

**Why the limit is the best over f:** the hack and its weaken cost the same per dollar at any share f, and grow costs more as f grows, so the cheapest batch is the one at f → 0.

**The batcher ceiling** divides the grow and weaken terms by c too. On zer0 at hacking 150 that is about 68% of the hacking ceiling. A whole-thread HWGW batch at f = 5–10% comes within 15% of the batcher ceiling, and never above it.

The script RAM is the game's (RamCostGenerator.ts:11-20). A host's usable RAM counts whole threads (`usableRamGb`): floor(RAM/1.7) threads at 1.75 GB each, at most its RAM, so a 2 GB host gives 1.75 GB.

**What both assume, and so ignore:**
- continuous batching with no gaps, every operation landing on time, and fractional threads within a host;
- security and money held at their best;
- the hosts' whole RAM free, and the best cores among them applied to all of it (loose where cores differ);
- one target at a time;
- the hacking level as the snapshot has it.

**BitNode multipliers:** `ScriptHackMoney` and `ServerGrowthRate` are folded into the player's `hacking_money` and `hacking_grow` (#321, #334, core's `bitNodeHackingMultipliers`), from the BitNode the slow snapshots show: on the dashboard the newest's, in the run report the newest by each medium snapshot's time; with no slow snapshot, the ceiling is unknown, never BN1's. So the ceilings are exact in BN1, BN2, BN4 and BN5. Elsewhere `ScriptHackMoneyGain` and `HackingSpeedMultiplier` are taken as 1 (and `ServerGrowthRate` outside those four), and are at most 1, so the ceilings err high, the safe side. BN11's `ServerWeakenRate` of 2 errs low (BitNode.tsx:896).

**Over the snapshots** (`hackingCeiling`, `src/hacking-ceiling.ts`). The targets are the game's servers rooted, with money:
- **The score** uses the RAM the brain's hacking uses: the hack loop's hosts (`chooseHackHosts`, `hackLoopHosts`'s names: the cloud servers among them since #194, and A7's later), and with `batcher.home` on (#340's review) home's RAM beyond what it keeps (`homeKeptGbOf`, with the Singularity worker of the slow snapshot then), as the batcher has it; the run report reads the switch as the store has it now. Home's cores are left out (a ceiling a little low there). It gives the best target's batcher ceiling, and beside it the hacking ceiling.
- **The bound** uses all the RAM a script could run on: every rooted server, including home and the cloud and Hacknet servers. It gives the best target's hacking ceiling. The owner's scripts, a terminal `hack`, or A7 on home all count in what hacking earns, so all that RAM counts here.
- **Unknown, with the reason,** when either snapshot is missing, or the player's skills (`hacking`, `intelligence`) or multipliers (`hacking_chance`, `hacking_speed`, `hacking_money`, `hacking_grow`).
- **Each target's batcher income on the hosts' RAM** (#275, given `incomes`): its batcher ceiling a GB times the hosts' RAM, at most what batches can take from its money (core's `batchIncomeMostPerSecond`). The run report keeps them a medium snapshot at a time (`HackingFold`'s `targetIncomes`, by index into one list of names: a 14-hour store's 5,000 medium snapshots), for section 6's stuck target check.

**What hacking earned:** the slow snapshot's `moneySources` (#196), which is `ns.getMoneySources().sinceInstall`. Its `hacking` field counts every hack's money (NetscriptHelpers.tsx:599).
- **Between slow snapshots** it is the rise in that count.
- **After an install** (`resetInfo.lastAugReset` changed; the install resets the count), what the count says is what was earned since.
- **A fall with no install** is a relaunch, which loads the save's older count. That interval counts as nothing earned.
- **Why not money's rises:** they would count crime and work, and purchases would hide hacking.

**The dashboard's rows:** "Hacking ceiling" and "Hacking income". The income row shows the last 10 minutes as percentages of both ceilings now. The brain sends their text, the same words the run report uses.

**The run report's section 5** (`HackingFold`, fed by the run report's own pass over the store: static, slow, then medium snapshots):
- **The ceilings:** each medium snapshot's ceilings, with the static snapshot in force then, stepped across each interval between slow snapshots. A window that starts after the Agent's static snapshot takes the one before it (`latestSnapshotBefore`).
- **The headline is the best 10 minutes:** hacking's percentage of the batcher ceiling, the stretch A7 is judged on. Preparing a target and switching between targets mean no run comes near 100% over a whole window.
- **Then the window and each hour** against both ceilings, and the ceilings now.
- **Under each hour, what held the cycle back** (#245, `BitNodeCycles.heldBackBetween`; under the window's line with an hour or less): 2b's judgements made in the hour, by time online, the most first, answers tied for the most named together ("reputation and factions equally"). Run #3's BN1.2 read as a failure at "0.1% of the batcher ceiling" for hours in which reputation held the cycle back and up to $766,000,000,000,000 went unspent. Where the most is reputation, the count or the brain's own state, it says income wasn't it, with the most money the judgements giving that answer saw (each judgement's own medium figure, so an hour across a destroy never quotes the BitNode before's): "held back by reputation, not income (92% of 55 min online judged in the hour; count 8%); most money unspent $766,000,000,000,000". Where money is among them, "held back by money: income (…)" ("reputation and money: income"). "factions" makes neither claim, as joining one can cost a travel. Or "not judged in the hour" and why. A judgement counts in the hour it was made, its time online to the next judgement with it.
- **Not judged:** intervals where the ceilings were unknown; the section says for how long.
- **A failure:** hacking earned more over the window than the bound's $ allow. The bound's $ are the ceiling on all rooted RAM, plus what the game's servers' money fell by between relaunches and installs (money they held at the start, hacked and not grown back, which no RAM paid for). This means the formula, or the measure, is wrong.
- **Not allowed for, known slack:** security left above where it started, which is the same kind of debt; and hacks finishing early in the window that started before it, a few minutes' worth.
- **A floor since #206:** the score suite (`test/batcher-score.test.ts`) holds the batcher to `LEAST_SCORE`, 24.5% of the batcher ceiling, and above the hack loop on the same seed.

**Tests:**
- **`packages/core/test/hacking-ceiling.test.ts`:**
  - chance, time, the bonuses and both per-dollar costs by hand on zer0, and their edges;
  - each multiplier and the cores;
  - a batch at shares from 0.9 to 0.0001 always below the hacking ceiling, rising towards it;
  - whole-thread HWGW batches at 5–10% below the batcher ceiling and within 15% of it;
  - `usableRamGb`.
- **`test/hacking-ceiling-parity.test.ts`:** in the simulator, with Formulas.exe bought through the simulator's own effectors:
  - core's chance, share, times, growth log and weaken against `ns.formulas.hacking`, over four servers and two players;
  - the security per thread and the scripts' RAM against the game's own;
  - the batcher ceiling above whole-thread HWGW batches sized with the game's own `growThreads`;
  - core's `growThreads`, the batcher's (#204), equal to the game's `formulas.hacking.growThreads` over the same servers, players, shares and cores.
- **`test/hacking-ceiling.test.ts`:**
  - the score's hosts and the bound's rooted servers, cores, multipliers and unknowns;
  - income rises, relaunches and installs;
  - the step across medium snapshots;
  - the section's lines and the best 10 minutes;
  - the failure and its allowance, split at relaunches;
  - no time judged, and the order the snapshots are fed in;
  - each target's batcher income on the hosts' RAM (#275): the RAM's figure capped by the target's money, the ceiling unchanged by it, and the fold's one in force at a time (none before the first, from one over 30 s old, or from before an install between), a target new or no longer rooted, and no best with nothing hackable.
- **`test/run-report.test.ts`, `test/dashboard-status.test.ts`, `test/dashboard-page.test.ts`, `test/telemetry-store.test.ts`:** the section, its FAIL, a relaunch that isn't one, a window after the static snapshot, and the rows.

### The ceiling suite

`test/hacking-ceiling-simulation.test.ts` (#196) runs the brain from an 8 GB fresh start in BN1, with the loop, the programs, home RAM and, since #194, cloud servers on, for 90 virtual minutes, past the loop's move from n00dles. The table below was measured before #194. Cloud servers start 82 to 99 minutes in (the [cloud server suite](#the-cloud-server-suite)), so seed 1's figures are unchanged (checked); seeds whose first purchase comes before 90 minutes (2 and 4 to 6) may differ in their last minutes.
- **The run report's section 5** gives the best 10 minutes, the window and the hours, with no failure.
- **Read directly:** hacking never earns more than the hacking ceiling on the loop's hosts, over the run, each hour or each 10 minutes; the batcher ceiling ≤ the hacking ceiling ≤ the bound.
- **In CI** it runs seed 1, in about 10 s.

**The hack loop's percentages,** the baseline the batcher (A7) must beat. Set `HACKING_CEILING_SEEDS` to `1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8`, as for the income suite; it takes about 75 s.

| Seed | Over the run, of the batcher ceiling | Over the run, of the hacking ceiling | Best 10 min, of the batcher ceiling (of the hacking ceiling) | Ceilings at the end (batcher / hacking / bound) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **1.8%** | 1.2% | **19.8%** (15.6%) | $2.74m/s / $3.81m/s on 1500 GB / $4.14m/s on 1628 GB |
| 2 | **5.3%** | 3.8% | **19.9%** (15.7%) | $3.19m/s / $4.39m/s on 1596 GB / $5.10m/s |
| 3 | **2.8%** | 2.0% | **19.0%** (15.0%) | $2.48m/s / $3.44m/s on 1316 GB / $4.12m/s |
| 4 | **5.2%** | 3.7% | **19.7%** (15.7%) | $3.62m/s / $4.96m/s on 1740 GB / $5.69m/s |
| 5 | **5.5%** | 3.9% | **19.3%** (15.2%) | $3.53m/s / $4.85m/s on 1748 GB / $5.57m/s |
| 6 | **4.9%** | 3.4% | **19.6%** (15.5%) | $2.64m/s / $3.66m/s on 1420 GB / $4.32m/s |
| 7 | **1.7%** | 1.2% | **18.3%** (14.4%) | $2.42m/s / $3.37m/s on 1324 GB / $3.69m/s |
| 8 | **2.0%** | 1.4% | **18.7%** (14.8%) | $1.90m/s / $2.64m/s on 1012 GB / $2.97m/s |

**Reading the table:**
- **Over the run,** the loop reaches 1.7% to 5.5% of the batcher ceiling.
- **Its best 10 minutes, 18% to 20%,** always come in the first minutes, on n00dles with a few small hosts.
- **The batcher's best target is phantasy** at the end of every seed.
- **Why the loop is so far below:** it hacks at 75% of max money and up to 5 over min security, with a 2.4 GB script whose threads mostly wait, and it prepares every target from 4% of its money.
## The batcher

M4 phase A slices A7b and A7c ([#205](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/205), [#206](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/206); the batcher itself is #204's, [packages/ingame](../ingame/README.md#the-batcher)). `src/batcher.ts`, sent by `DesiredStateController`.
- **The switch:** the dashboard's "Stop the batcher", on by default since #206 ([owner decision 49](../../docs/decisions/owner-decisions.md): "On by default"), with the hack loop's switch on. It was off in #205. The owner switches it off on the dashboard, and the hack loop takes its servers back.
- **The mode, from the RAM:** the batcher when a host holds it and a plan on the hosts' RAM earns; otherwise the hack loop (no host with the batcher's 3.85 GB free beyond what home keeps for the Agent, no figures, nothing it can hack), and the dashboard says why. The brain logs each change of mode, of target and of the next target ("batcher: batches n00dles, preparing joesguns next", "batcher: the hack loop hacks, not the batcher: no host holds the batcher").
- **Its hosts:** all the hack loop's; the hack loop is sent on with its target and no hosts, so its loops stop there, and it still roots. And since [#340](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/340) home, last, for its RAM beyond what it keeps for the Agent and its workers: `homeKeptGbOf` (32 GB, `SHARE_HOME_KEPT_GB`, and with Singularity its largest worker's at the SF4 level owned), the one reserve the share and levelling mode keep, and the batcher's own budget where it runs there. So home is a host from about 36 GB, about 70 in BN4 (32 + 33.9 + 3.85). **While sharing is asked,** home stays a host only if the batches' income with it gains more, relatively, than the share would on home's RAM (`homeBatchesWhileSharing`, share.ts: income with home / income without, less 1, above core's `shareBonus` on home's threads and cores, less 1, from two `planBatcher` calls on the target chosen): **a placeholder** until the planner splits home's RAM between batching, levelling and sharing (owner decision 67). In run #3's late shape (64 TB home beside 1.6 PB of hosts) home shares; after an install, with home most of the RAM, it batches. The mode line says "home sharing rather than batching (#340's placeholder)" while it shares. With home among the hosts, the config says whether its plan is held to the RAM (`ramBound`, core's `isPlanRamBound`: below the share and spacing unbounded RAM would give), which the batcher echoes in its health for the run report. The config's `homeKeptGb` says what home keeps, and the batcher leaves it free beside all that runs there ([packages/ingame](../ingame/README.md#the-batcher)). Left out of the plan, and labelled: what the Agent's own scripts use on home beside what it keeps (optimistic: the batcher fits each batch to the RAM free as it goes, #232, so at most that much is trimmed, and only on a home small beside it), and home's cores (cautious: a grow or weaken there is stronger, `Server/formulas/grow.ts:25`, `NetscriptFunctions.ts:367`). Before #340, under owner decision 34 ("home is left to the Agent"), batches never ran on home, which was 34.7% of BN4.1's rooted RAM and 80 to 100% of it in the first hour after each install. `batcher.home` (`BATCHER_ON_HOME_SETTING`, on by default, not on the dashboard) turns it off: the A/B's arm without it, below.
- **Where it runs:** `planBatcher` puts the batcher on home when home's RAM leaves its budget (`BATCHER_RAM_BUDGET_GB`, 3.85) beyond the 8 GB home keeps for the Agent (so from 16 GB), else on the largest host, whose RAM it then counts that much less; kept where it runs while it still fits there (#211), since a move restarts it.
- **Its target (#206, `chooseBatcher`):** of the game's servers rooted with money that a hack takes something from (`batcherTargetsOf`), the `BATCHER_TARGETS_PLANNED` (5) best by A1's batcher ceiling, the one sent, its next and the one it batches now, each planned on the hosts' RAM with core's `batchPlan`. Since #274 also every target prepared now (only a prepared one can be moved to while one earns, so every one is planned, whatever its rank), and the steps: the 5 best by the ceiling of those that might be the next (#278's review), that a move from the one batched can reach (a weaken at their minimum within `BATCHER_MOST_IDLE_MS` of its; with nothing batched, a weaken within it, as a target chosen then must earn within it), that earn within `BATCHER_NEXT_HORIZON_MS` on the next's RAM, and that would be worth the move and their cost: checked at twice A1's ceiling first (no plan beats it), then on their plan, made as each is met, so one that fails on its plan takes no step's place (#278's re-review); on a move, or with nothing batched, the steps from the one chosen too. In BN1.3's exit run the 5 best by the ceiling were all at 3 times their minimum security after an install, none could be moved to or be the next, and the mid targets that led to them were never weighed: about 5 hours on n00dles or phantasy at hacking 1,300 to 1,700. A plan costs 0.1 to 4 ms on BN1's richest at hacking 1,300, but 365 ms on one whose hacking level is the player's, so the brain doesn't plan them all. A target's worth is its plan's income over the next `BATCHER_TARGET_HORIZON_MS` (15 minutes), less the time until it earns: preparing it from its money and security in the medium snapshot (core's `preparationTimeMs`, wave by wave, each a weaken's time at the security then), on the RAM the batched target's batches leave (#216's review), then a weaken's time for its first batch to land. The one batched now earns at once. A target needing more hacking than the player has isn't one: a hack takes nothing.
- **Never long without income (#216's review, #184's lesson):** with nothing batched (a fresh start, a handover, a restart), a target is chosen only if it earns within `BATCHER_MOST_IDLE_MS` (5 minutes, half of `incomeStalled`'s 10), or with none such, the soonest to earn; while one earns, another only once it's prepared (#236, below). Of those, the worthiest, but the one sent is kept unless another is worth `BATCHER_MOVE_GAIN` (1.25) times it, as a move costs.
- **Earning through a move (#236):** while a target earns (the one batched), another is chosen only once it's prepared (at its minimum security and maximum money in the medium snapshot): its first batch is then a weaken away, and the batches out land meanwhile. A better target that still needs preparing, and that a move could reach once it's prepared, is the next instead, prepared beside the batches, and the move comes once it's ready. A prepared one is chosen if its first batch lands within `BATCHER_MOST_IDLE_MS` of the batches out (its weaken less theirs: #237's review, as a weaken can take over 5 minutes). Run #2 moved off an earning target to one still to prepare, and nothing new was batched while it was. In the simulator, a fresh start's first move (n00dles to phantasy, at about 50 minutes) now leaves 1.8–2.0 minutes without a hack over 8 seeds (a weaken of phantasy's, less n00dles' batches still landing), against 6.5 minutes before, when it moved to harakiri-sushi still to prepare and the batcher then waited for the next target's wave. Over the 8 seeds the batcher hacks $373b by 55 minutes, against $105b before, though 1% less by 40 minutes (10% on seed 4): the next target's fifth of the RAM is now really used.
- **The next target:** of the rest worth `BATCHER_MOVE_GAIN` times the chosen one's worth once prepared (its income over the horizon less a batch's weaken; against the chosen one's worth once batched, as the later move to it will be, so on a move a next isn't sent that the move to it would refuse: #278's re-review), the move's own test, and that a move from the chosen one could reach once prepared (#274: a weaken at its minimum within `BATCHER_MOST_IDLE_MS` of its), the one worth most over `BATCHER_NEXT_HORIZON_MS` (60 minutes, #274): its income less the time to prepare it on `BATCHER_NEXT_RAM_SHARE` (a fifth) of the RAM, or what the batches leave (below), and for a batch to land. One that earns nothing in that hour isn't the next, nor one whose gain over the chosen one after it earns, over the hour, is less than the chosen one's batches lose meanwhile on four fifths of the RAM (#278's review: nothing where they leave a fifth idle, as n00dles' do on a large RAM; a RAM-bound target loses up to a fifth of its income while the next is prepared). Before #274 it had to be prepared within the 15-minute horizon, ranked by its worth once prepared: after an install BN1's richest are at 3 times their minimum security, where one weaken took 17 to 32 minutes in BN1.3's exit run, so none was ever the next. Preparing the next costs the batches at most its fifth of the RAM, and nothing where they leave it idle, so an hour is the conservative bound the owner may change: it meets every first batch of the run's richest (23 to 44 minutes), and a cycle there ran 28 minutes to 2.3 hours. That holds at BN1.3's hacking speed (about 2.4 times); at speed 1, ecorp's first batch after an install is about 102 minutes away, but the reach binds first, and steps lead there. A next a move can't reach is never sent: before #274 one was prepared, kept and never moved to while the chosen one earned (#237's re-review). So a target worth a path from the one batched (worth the move once prepared, reachable, earning within the hour and worth its cost) gets a move or a next: from n00dles to BN1's richest, a mid target first (`test/batcher-choice.property.test.ts`). **The limit that remains:** a richer target whose weaken is more than `BATCHER_MOST_IDLE_MS` past the batched one's is reached only through a chain of steps, each worth a path from the last; with no such chain, it isn't (at speed 1 and hacking 1,300, alpha-ent's weaken is 6.9 minutes and clarkinc's 12.3: from n00dles, clarkinc is a chain away). In the 23:00 shape of BN1.3's exit run (hacking 1,327, 424 TB, synthetic), the brain now moves from n00dles ($20m/s) to rho-construction, prepared ($31b/s), with clarkinc as the next, then to clarkinc ($294b/s); before, n00dles throughout. It goes in the config as `next`, with that RAM; the batches are planned on the rest. Since #237's review, that RAM is all the chosen target's batches leave, a fifth at least (late in a cycle n00dles' batches leave most of the RAM idle), while it's the one earning; with nothing batched yet (a fresh start, after an install or a restart, a move under way), a fifth, as the chosen target prepares on the rest (#237's re-review). So while one earns, any target the rule before #236 moved to directly, prepared on the RAM the batches leave within 5 minutes, is preparable as the next: none is left without a path. The batcher prepares it beside them, and once it's ready the same test moves to it, so its RAM never sits idle (#216's review). **The next sent is kept** unless another is worth `BATCHER_MOVE_GAIN` times it over that hour (#236; once prepared, before #274), even if it no longer earns within the hour or gains enough while it's still worth the move and reachable, and the RAM will ever prepare it (#278's re-review) (#278's review: hosts lost, as at 01:00 in BN1.3's exit run, 424 to 261 TB): a next dropped wastes its waves out, whose RAM is held for a weaken's time at its security (run #2's next went megacorp, ecorp, then none, in 11 minutes; the simulator's harakiri-sushi, max-hardware, phantasy in 7).
- **After an install,** a health written before the game's last `lastAugReset` isn't news (#216's review): ports outlive an install, and a batcher the reset killed would still read as batching.
- **Its share and spacing:** core's `batchPlan` at the medium snapshot's hacking level and multipliers, planned again at each snapshot (every 10 s), so the plan follows the level as it rises; the batcher reads each change hot (architecture §5.5).
- **The dashboard's "Batcher" lines:** what was sent (where it runs, the target, the hosts, the share and the spacing) or why nothing, and its health from the latest fast snapshot: its state, batches, hacks landed, failed and skipped, desyncs (out of order, out of bounds), late, lost and short, and any problem; red with a desync or a problem. Its alerts: `batcherNotRunning` (#211) and `batcherOutOfStep` (#206), above.
- **The run report:** section 5's best 10 minutes say who hacked in them ("by: the batcher, batching on n00dles"): the batcher's percentage of A1's measure (#206). Section 6 has the batcher's health in the fast snapshots, hour by hour (each run's counts added up, a restart's from 0), then its desyncs, late and lost, the least slack any operation had when it was called (#216's review: one late operation fails the run, so the margin shows), the batches short of RAM (and of them, those while sharing on the batches' hosts, the share's; each short the health keeps listed once, #246: when, the operation and its threads, how long it waited, the hosts' free RAM, and what held the rest: its own operations past their landing, scripts not its own, its share threads), the operations that waited for RAM at their time, the longest wait in the run so far, and each wait the health keeps with what held the RAM and how late the landings then ran (#246; "N more not kept" past the 5 kept between snapshots; "not reported" for an older batcher), and those trimmed to it (#232: fewer hack threads than the plan's, or none, income lost but not a failure; and of them, those the share's threads on the batches' hosts made smaller, the share's `share starved batches`), the money after each batch's grow (#234: "full at all N looks", or the looks below the target's maximum with the run's least so far and its target, a grow short of the hack before it, so the batches drift toward the bounds' floor; and the grows not looked at, #235's review; "not reported" for an older batcher; shown, not a failure, since one that reaches the floor is a desync), the level at the hacks' landings (#315: the hacks sent smaller for the level forecast at their landing, and the most levels forecast between a hack's start and its landing; "not reported" for an older batcher), home among its hosts (#340: what home may give the batches and what they held there while batching, and `batches left home idle`, below), each move with its idle time (#236: "n00dles → phantasy at …: idle 1.9 min", from the last hack on the target before to the first on the new one, from the health's `lastMove`; a move under way at a run's last snapshot "not done at …: idle … so far"; a move finished before the window isn't listed (#237's review: the health keeps it until the next); a move across a batcher's restart, whose new run starts with no last hack, is joined, ", across a restart", from the latest last hack of the runs before (#237's re-review: runs with none between, a crash loop, don't break it), but never across an install (the slow snapshots' `lastAugReset`), when the cycle starts from nothing; a move under way is the health's `targetChangedSinceHack` (#237's re-review: a detour back to the target last hacked shows too); "not reported" for an older batcher), and the problems it named. A run already under way at the window's start counts from there (#211). **A move idle past `BATCHER_MOVE_IDLE_FAILS_AFTER_MS` is a failure (#236):** 10 minutes, twice what the brain allows a move, done or not, so a repeat of run #2's 25 minutes can't hide inside "income stalled". **A batcher stuck on poor targets is a warning for an hour, and a failure for 2 hours in a cycle (#275):** a target is poor while the best rooted target would earn 100 times more (`BATCHER_POOR_TARGET_RATIO`), each on the hack loop's hosts' RAM by the medium snapshot then (none from one over 30 s old, or from before an install between it and the look): A1's batcher ceiling a GB times that RAM, at most what batches can take from the target's money (core's `batchIncomeMostPerSecond`, a batch every 80 ms at the largest share a plan takes), so n00dles' $1.75m caps it whatever the RAM. A GB alone doesn't see that: at hacking 1,300, n00dles' batcher ceiling a GB is only about 70 times under the best's, but on 424 TB its plan earned $19.5m/s against b-and-a's $818b/s (#274). A stretch is the batcher's health on poor targets, one or several, its time the time seen (looks no more than 6 s apart, both poor). An install ends it, and so does 10 minutes or more off poor targets (`BATCHER_STUCK_BREAK_MS`: from its last poor look to the next, with a look between judged under 100 times or on a target that isn't poor); a shorter dip or move joins the stretches either side, its time not counted (#277's review: a minute's step off and back can't split 3 hours into pieces under an hour). A look with an income unknown, or a gap, neither ends nor adds to one. A cycle with 2 hours on poor targets in all, every stretch's whatever its length (`BATCHER_STUCK_FAILS_AFTER_MS`, owner decision 59: decision 55's gate, a report failure), fails in one line with its time and its longest stretch; in the others, each stretch of an hour or more (`BATCHER_STUCK_WARNS_AFTER_MS`: a target at 3 times its minimum security after an install is one weaken from prepared, 17 to 33 minutes at hacking 1,300, and the next is prepared beside the batches) is a warning. Each names the targets, the cycle (2b's), the range of ratios over the stretch and the best target with both incomes at the largest (the least is about 100 times wherever the RAM grew across the line, so it alone would read a gross trap as a marginal one), and the hacking levels. Over the BN1.3 exit run's store it names cycle 6 (phantasy, 1 h 19 min, WARN), cycle 7 (n00dles, 2 h 9 min, FAIL) and cycle 8 (n00dles, 1 h 9 min, WARN); that run's report said PASS. **Out of step is a failure (#206):** any desync (a landing out of order, or the target's money under the floor or security over the ceiling at a hack), late or lost operation fails the run, with the counts and the first snapshot that showed one, unless a page stall (or, for a batcher before #251, a clock step) explains it and the batcher recovered by itself: a warning (#241, owner decision 55). The batcher sent but not running for 3 fast snapshots in a row or more is a failure too (#211), each stretch with its reason. **So is a batch short of RAM (#232)**, except while sharing on the batches' hosts, which is the share's `share starved batches`: the batcher fits each batch to the RAM as it will be held, so one short is RAM taken after it was planned (anyone else's script on its hosts), RAM freed later than the 100 ms an operation waits for it (#246: a landing running late on a busy page), or the fit is wrong, and its hack is lost.
- **Tests:** `test/batcher.test.ts` (the switch, the plan, where it runs, the target and the next, the move and the fallback, the 23:00 shape of BN1.3's exit run and the path off n00dles (#274), what's weighed beyond the 5 best by the ceiling, the steps, the next's cost and bound, a next kept past the hour, and what's planned on 60 targets (#274, #278's review), the controller with it on and with nothing to plan and its log, the view, the alert's facts, the report's section, its failures and warnings); `test/batcher-choice.property.test.ts` (#274): over random shapes (the player's level and speed, up to 14 targets' figures, money and security, 1 to 4 hosts of 4 TB to 1 PB) from n00dles batched, a target worth a path (worth its cost too) gets a move or a next, each move is to a target prepared within `BATCHER_MOST_IDLE_MS`, each next is one a move can reach and earns within the hour, and following the choices comes to rest where none is worth a path; a floor test on seed 274 counts the shapes whose move or next is beyond the 5 best by the ceiling, those needing a chain of moves, and those resting with a richer target and no chain; `test/batcher-simulation.test.ts`, the brain with the switch on from an 8 GB fresh start for 20 virtual minutes of batching: every fast snapshot's health in the store in step, hacks landing, the hack loop's loops gone from the batcher's hosts and ours running once where the brain said, section 6 clean; turned off, ours stops and the loops come back; a fresh start's first move (#236): seed 1 moves from n00dles to phantasy, prepared beside its batches, at about 50 minutes, under 3 minutes idle (1.9), and section 6 shows it with nothing failed; seed 1 in CI, `BATCHER_AGENT_SEEDS` (`1,2,3,4`, say) for others. E2 runs the batcher on the real page ([packages/e2](../e2/README.md#what-the-tests-show)).

### The score suite (#206)

`test/batcher-score.test.ts`: an 8 GB fresh start, the brain as on Steam (the batcher on, by default), for 30 virtual minutes, then the same seed with the batcher off. Each is scored as the run report scores a live run: the best 10 minutes of what hacking earned against A1's batcher ceiling. The batcher must beat the hack loop on every seed by `LEAST_TIMES_THE_LOOP` (1.4×, #206 as the owner amended it, decision 49), at least `LEAST_SCORE` (24.5%), with the report failing nothing (no desync, nothing late, lost or short). CI runs seed 1; `BATCHER_SCORE_SEEDS=1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8` runs all 8 (about 75 s):

| Seed | The batcher's best 10 min | From | The hack loop's best 10 min | From | Times |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **29.0%** | 4.7 min | 17.2% | 2.7 min | 1.69 |
| 2 | **29.0%** | 4.7 min | 17.2% | 2.7 min | 1.68 |
| 3 | **29.2%** | 8.7 min | 16.9% | 3.7 min | 1.73 |
| 4 | **28.0%** | 5.7 min | 19.9% | 1.7 min | 1.41 |
| 5 | **29.6%** | 8.7 min | 16.3% | 1.7 min | 1.82 |
| 6 | **30.2%** | 4.7 min | 17.3% | 3.7 min | 1.75 |
| 7 | **28.7%** | 5.7 min | 16.1% | 1.7 min | 1.78 |
| 8 | **28.9%** | 4.7 min | 16.1% | 1.7 min | 1.80 |

The table is since #232. At #206, before the batcher counted each host a thread short and fitted each batch to the RAM, the batcher's were 27.5–30.7%, 1.48–1.84 times (29.5% on average, 29.1% now). Seed 4's 1.41× is just over the 1.4× floor: the seeds swing about ±1.5 points, so a change to the batcher runs all 8 and reports seed 4's margin. #236 left the table as it was: the best 10 minutes all come before the first move.

**Reading the table:**
- **The batcher beats the hack loop on every seed, 1.41 to 1.82 times:** #206 asked for 2 times, and the owner amended it to 1.4 times (2026-10-29, decision 49). Its best 10 minutes are all on n00dles, early, every batch in step.
- **Why not more, early:** A1's ceiling is the best rooted target's. Within minutes of a fresh start that's joesguns, then harakiri-sushi, which a fresh start's RAM can't prepare: growing joesguns from its 4% takes about 4,600 grow threads (8.3 TB at once), against the 100 to 460 GB it has. So the batcher batches n00dles, about 55% of the better target's ceiling on that RAM, less the weaken's time each jump in RAM takes to reach income.
- **Why not more, later:** once the batcher earns, the cloud server rule (#194) buys RAM so fast that the ceiling doubles every couple of minutes (100 GB at 9 minutes, 30 TB at 45), while new RAM earns only a weaken's time after the batches it holds start: on a target with a 1.5 minute weaken that alone keeps the batcher near a third of the ceiling. On 26 PB, the most cloud servers hold, one target's money caps the batcher far below a ceiling that grows with the RAM.
- **The money:** in 45 virtual minutes the batcher hacked $1.7b to $2.8b on seeds 1 to 8 (#206's exploration runs).
- **Short batches and skipped hacks:** at #206 a handful per seed (4 to 10 in 45 minutes, of over a thousand batches), when the plan grew with the RAM and an operation found the hosts' RAM still held by the batches planned before. Since #232 none: each batch is fitted to the RAM as it will be held, trimmed (fewer hack threads, or none that time) rather than short, and a short one fails the run report. Waves of preparation leave the RAM the batches' orders still need, and a first batch after a preparation wave starts beside it only with room for the batches' peak.

### Home among its hosts: the A/B (#340)

`test/home-batch-host-simulation.test.ts` (owner decision 67, point 6): the first virtual hour after an install, with home large and no cloud servers yet, as BN4.1's cycles started. A harness edit stands in for the cycles before: home of 4 TB before the Agent; the rest is an install's (hacking from level 1, $1,000, no programs). The brain's rules as on Steam, the programs bought with the money; cloud servers, home RAM and augmentations off. Each seed runs twice, with home among the batcher's hosts and without (`batcher.home` off: main's before #340), about 14 s of real time each. It checks the config, home's RAM in the batches, the money, and the run report's home line with no `batches left home idle`. CI runs seed 1; `HOME_BATCH_SEEDS=1,2,3,4` runs others, `HOME_BATCH_MINUTES` another length.

| Seed | Without home | With home | Times | The hour's money without, reached with home at | Home in the batches while batching | The share on average, without → with | Reputation gained, without → with |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | $1,684.5m | $17,520.7m | **10.40** | 24.3 min | 86% of the 4,064 GB it may give | ×1.202 → ×1.000 | 2,896 → **3,562** |
| 2 | $2,904.5m | $25,407.3m | **8.75** | 31.5 min | 87% | ×1.202 → ×1.000 | 2,981 → **3,675** |
| 3 | $1,650.1m | $20,667.6m | **12.53** | 28.3 min | 88% | ×1.197 → ×1.000 | 2,807 → **3,604** |
| 4 | $2,868.8m | $24,982.6m | **8.71** | 31.3 min | 88% | ×1.197 → ×1.000 | 2,899 → **3,669** |

Faction work is on by default, so the share was asked in 71–73% of the fast snapshots without home and 85% with it. The placeholder rule (`homeBatchesWhileSharing`) keeps home batching through all of it: the income gain is several times over, against a share of about ×1.28 on home's RAM (1,015 threads: 1 + ln(1,016)/25 = ×1.277). The arm without home shares home at ×1.2 on average; the arm with home has none, and still gains 19–28% more reputation (the factions' sum from 0, CyberSec and NiteSec), most likely from its higher hacking level (251–259 against 212–224), which hacking work's reputation rises with.

Without home, the batches had the game's 43 hosts and ended the hour on harakiri-sushi at hacking 212 to 224; with home, phantasy at 251 to 259. Left out: cloud servers bought within the hour (optimistic for home's gain: they'd add RAM to both arms) and installed augmentations' multipliers (cautious: a slower level reaches the richer targets, whose batches want the RAM, later). The bars: more than 4 times the money, the hour's money of the arm without within 45 minutes, and over half of what home may give in the batches.

## Sharing spare RAM

M4 phase A ([#224](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/224), epic #191): `ns.share()` raises the reputation all faction work earns while its threads run (10 s a call): hacking work's by its bonus, 1 + ln(1 + the threads × an intelligence and a core bonus)/25, field and security work's hacking and intelligence term only, with SF15.3's charisma in field's (`NetworkShare/Share.ts`, `PersonObjects/formulas/reputation.ts:16-52`; core's [share](../core/README.md#sharing-spare-ram)). The first overnight run (2026-10-31) ended with $157t unspent, reputation the bottleneck, and most of its 26 PB idle. `src/share.ts`, sent by `DesiredStateController`; the batcher does the sharing ([packages/ingame](../ingame/README.md#the-batcher)).
- **The switch:** the dashboard's "Stop sharing spare RAM" (`share.enabled`, `POST /api/share`), on by default ([owner decision 52](../../docs/decisions/owner-decisions.md)).
- **When:** while the player works for a faction: the work sent (the owner's on the dashboard, or the brain's own, #203) is faction work and the Agent's fast snapshot says the game showed it (`factionWorkGoingOn`; the game's `getPlayer()` has no current work, NetscriptFunctions.ts:1417-1436, so work the owner starts in the game by hand, with none set, isn't seen), with the batcher sent. With the batcher off, nothing shares: the hack loop's hosts are its own. The brain adds `sharing` to the batcher's config; the work over, it's gone at the next medium snapshot, and the batcher's last round ends within 10 s. Work stopped by hand is seen at the Agent's next look, every 30 s, so sharing can outlast it by about 40 s. Only during faction work is the project's choice: the game's bonus also raises passive and offline reputation (`FactionHelpers.tsx:132-170`).
- **The reserve** (`sharingConfigOf`, core's `shareReserveGb`): the batches' peak on the plan sent (core's `batchPeakRamGb`, at the medium snapshot's hacking level) 1.25 times, plus the next target's RAM, which the batcher keeps of its hosts' free RAM; what home keeps free beside them (`homeKeptGbOf`, the batches' reserve on home too since #340), `SHARE_HOME_KEPT_GB` (32 GB, 4 times `HOME_KEPT_FOR_THE_AGENT_GB`: a page backdoor's worker can outlive its round), plus, with Singularity (`singularityRamMultiplierOf`, #242's review), the most its largest worker (core's `SINGULARITY_LARGEST_WORKER_GB`, the destroy's 33.9 GB at SF4.3) takes at the SF4 level owned (×1, ×4 or ×16: 65.9 GB in all at SF4.3, 550.4 GB at SF4.1), not all of home, whose workers the Agent starts only in home's free RAM; and the cores over 1 of the hosts and home, for the bonus. Home shares beside the hosts (#226's review): after an install, before the cloud servers are bought again, it's most of the RAM. The free RAM already leaves out what the batches in flight hold, so the reserve counts them twice: the share is short, never a batch. Its bonus is logarithmic, so half the threads cost only ln 2/25 of it.
- **The rate:** the batcher's health reports its threads, their RAM and the bonus (core's `shareBonus`); the faction rule counts that bonus while threads run (`shareBonusOf`), in its choice, its ETA and the stall's rate ([factions and their work](#factions-and-their-work)), the World Daemon's path in The Red Pill's, and since [#345](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/345) A6's estimates by work (the planner's τ short of Daedalus's count, #243's hold: `countablesToCome`), at its mean over the income's window (`shareBonusMean`); not from a health 20 s old by the game's clock, or a batcher reported not running (a stopped batcher's last health stays on its port). A faction keeps the work type worked unless another earns `WORK_TYPE_SWITCH_GAIN` (1.1) times as much, so the bonus, which field and security work get only part of, doesn't flip it.
- **The brain's log:** the batcher's mode line says it ("batcher: batches harakiri-sushi, sharing its spare RAM") and changes when it stops.
- **The run report** (section 6, `src/share-report.ts`): how long it shared, its threads, RAM and bonus at most and on average; the brain's faction work's reputation over slow snapshot intervals all shared and none shared, and their ratio; the late, lost and short operations while sharing on the batches' hosts, while home alone shares beside them, and while not (#230); a health the batcher hasn't written again for 20 s isn't counted as sharing; from a batcher since #242, the bonus while asked, time-weighted, against the bonus possible under the share's rules (its health's `possibleBonus`), and the time at ×1.000 while asked (from an older batcher, both said not judged); and three failures, `share starved batches` (batches short of RAM while sharing on the batches' hosts, or trimmed for its threads there, the batcher's own count since #232's review, on their own terms: late and lost are the batcher's out-of-step failure; a share on home alone can't take a batch's RAM, so batches short then are not the share's failure but the batcher's own, `batches short of RAM`, #230, #232; each run's counts taken as the batcher's section takes them, a run that starts in the window from 0; `sharingOf` counts all of an older batcher's share as on its hosts) `share idle` (asked while batching, the room for a thread free, no threads, for 10 minutes: its rounds don't start), and `share left home idle` (#242: asked, and over half of what home may share free, its RAM beyond what it keeps, from its health's `homeGb`, `homeFreeGb` and `homeKeptGb`, for 5 minutes, whatever the room says: run #3's room was 0 while 0.5–1 PB of home sat idle for 51 minutes). The batcher sends its health at least every 10 s while asked, so a quiet one is never read as stopped (#242's review).
- **Tests:** `test/share.test.ts` (the switch, when it's asked, the reserve, home's kept RAM and cores, Singularity, the bonus counted and a stale one not, the faction rule's rate, the work type kept, The Red Pill's rate, the controller and its view, the report's lines, a stopped batcher's, all three failures, and short while home alone shares, not a failure, #230; #242: the bonus while asked against the possible, the time at ×1.000, home left idle at exactly 5 minutes and not just under, half of what home may share, not asked or a gap not idle, an older batcher's lines not judged, a mix of older and newer, odd figures dropped, and Singularity's kept RAM at each SF4 level); `test/faction-work-parity.test.ts` checks core's bonus against the engine's `getSharePower()` and `formulas.reputation.sharePower` while 100,000 threads share, and the rate with it for each work type against `factionGains`; `test/share-simulation.test.ts`, the brain in the simulator at a hacking level of about 900 with 25 cloud servers of 1 PB and the owner's CyberSec hacking work: over 4 minutes sharing and 4 with the switch off, CyberSec's reputation rises 1.613 times faster sharing, the bonus the batcher reported on average (1.614, about 4.6m threads), within 2%, with nothing late, lost or short. CI runs seed 1; `SHARE_SEEDS` (`1,2,3`, say) runs others. `test/share-home-simulation.test.ts` (#242) is run #3's 12:22 scaled down: 25 cloud servers of 64 TB and a 64 TB home, the batches on 97% of the cloud servers; home shares 65,496 GB beside them on average, the bonus ×1.388 on average of ×1.388 possible (before #242, ×1.000: the 80% cap counted home with the batches' hosts), never ×1.000, nothing late, lost or short, and the report's lines with no share failure. That's home beside the batcher's hosts, run with `batcher.home` off since #340. A second arm runs the default: while faction work asks for the share, home's 4% more batch RAM loses to ×1.388 (`homeBatchesWhileSharing`, #340's review), so home shares beside the hosts just the same (65,496 GB, ×1.388 of ×1.388 possible), the brain's log saying "home sharing rather than batching (#340's placeholder)". Before the placeholder rule home batched there: 65,423 GB of its 65,504 in the batches and the share at ×1.000. CI runs seed 1; `SHARE_HOME_SEEDS` runs others.

## The hacking programs

M3's second slice ([#136](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/136)): the brain tells the Agent which hacking program to buy next, and the Agent buys it through the game's page ([`packages/ingame`](../ingame/README.md#the-hacking-programs)). The code is `src/programs.ts`.

- **The switch:** "Stop buying programs" on the dashboard ("Start buying programs" when off), on unless the owner turns it off (the store's `programs.enabled`), like the hack loop's.
- **The next item** (`chooseNextPurchase`), from the connected Agent's medium snapshot (`hasTorRouter`, `programs`, the player's money and city):
  - the first not owned, in the order TOR router, BruteSSH.exe, FTPCrack.exe, relaySMTP.exe, HTTPWorm.exe, SQLInject.exe;
  - once the player's money covers its price: $200k, $500k, $1.5m, $5m, $30m, $250m (`Constants.ts:44`, `DarkWeb/DarkWebItems.ts`);
  - the TOR router needs a tech vendor in the player's city, one per city that has any (`TECH_VENDORS` in core): Sector-12 Alpha Enterprises, Aevum NetLink Technologies, Ishima Omega Software, Volhaven CompuTek. Chongqing and New Tokyo have none, and the dashboard says travel is needed.
- **The dashboard** shows what's owned, what's being bought, or what it waits for (money for an item, or travel).
- **Sent as an intent** (#141): `buyTor { vendor }` or `buyProgram { program }`, in the one desired state, with the hack loop's rules: nothing new until the connected Agent's own snapshots; while the brain holds, only a state that starts nothing new (`desiredStateWhileHolding` in core: the hack loop off, or as last sent, and no intents).

## Home RAM

M3's third slice ([#138](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/138)): the brain tells the Agent when to double home's RAM, and the Agent buys it at a tech vendor through the game's page ([`packages/ingame`](../ingame/README.md#home-ram)). The code is `src/home-ram.ts`.

- **The switch:** "Stop upgrading home RAM" on the dashboard ("Start upgrading home RAM" when off), on unless the owner turns it off (the store's `homeRam.enabled`), like the others.
- **The next upgrade** (`chooseHomeRamUpgrade`), from the connected Agent's medium snapshot (home's `maxRam`, the player's money and city) and slow snapshot (the BitNode and its options):
  - home's RAM doubled, once the player's money covers the price: `ram × 32,000 × 1.58^log2(ram)` in BN1 (`PersonObjects/Player/PlayerObjectServerMethods.ts:30`), $1.01m for 8 GB to 16 GB, about 3.16× more each doubling;
  - **BN1, BN2, BN4 and BN5 only, for now** (#321, #323, #334, core's `bitNodePriceMultipliers`): BN2's, BN4's and BN5's `HomeComputerRamCost` is 1, BN1's price; other BitNodes multiply it, so there nothing is bought, and the dashboard says the price isn't known yet. Nothing either until the Agent's slow snapshot says which BitNode (its first round);
  - up to the game's limit, 2^30 GB, or 128 GB with the BitNode option `restrictHomePCUpgrade`;
  - at the tech vendor in the player's city (`TECH_VENDORS`, as for the TOR router). Chongqing and New Tokyo have none, and the dashboard says travel is needed.
- **One purchase at a time, cheapest first:** of the next program and the next upgrade, both affordable, the controller sends the cheaper, and the other's view says it waits for it. Money covers the cheaper one first, so nothing is bought ahead of it. With both switches on, the order is TOR, BruteSSH.exe, 16 GB, FTPCrack.exe, 32 GB, relaySMTP.exe, 64 GB, HTTPWorm.exe, 128 GB, 256 GB, SQLInject.exe, then the rest of the doublings. A switch that's off takes its activity out of the comparison. With home RAM on, no program goes either until the slow snapshot says which BitNode, since until then the upgrade's price isn't known. And the cheaper purchase holds back the other while the page can't make it (a collapsed World section, say), even a program the terminal could buy; the dashboard says what each waits for.
- **The dashboard** shows home's RAM, the upgrade being bought, or what it waits for (money, travel, the cheaper purchase, the BitNode).
- **Sent as an intent** (#141): `upgradeHomeRam { fromRam, vendor }`, with the programs' purchase or instead of it.

## Cloud servers

M4 phase A's A2 ([#194](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/194), epic #191, owner decision 46): the brain buys and upgrades cloud servers by a payback rule computed from the game's prices and the hack loop's measured income, and the hack loop runs on them ([the hosts](#the-hack-loop)). The Agent buys through the NS API, with no page ([`packages/ingame`](../ingame/README.md#buying-servers)). The code is `src/cloud-servers.ts`.

- **The switch:** "Stop buying cloud servers" on the dashboard ("Start buying cloud servers" when off), on unless the owner turns it off (the store's `cloudServers.enabled`), like the others. Nothing is bought with the hack loop switched off: cloud servers only run it.
- **The game's prices** (BN1, `Server/ServerPurchases.ts:23-54`, `Server/data/Constants.ts:4,12-13`): $55,000 a GB at every size (`CloudServerSoftcap` is 1), so an 8 GB server is $440k and 1 TB $56.3m; an upgrade costs the difference between the two sizes' prices, the same per GB added. At most 25 servers, of up to 2^20 GB. BN4's are the same but for `CloudServerSoftcap`, 1.2 (#321, `BitNode/BitNode.tsx:633-661`, `cloudServerPriceIn`): a GB above 64 GB costs 1.2 times more each doubling, so a bigger server pays back later. Other BitNodes multiply the price, the limit and the most RAM (`BitNode/BitNode.tsx`): there nothing is bought, and the dashboard says the prices aren't known yet. Nothing either until the Agent's slow snapshot says which BitNode.
- **Income per GB** (`HackLoopIncome`): from the connected Agent's medium snapshots, money's rises added up (a purchase lowers money, which isn't income), over the hack loop's hosts' RAM × time (`hackLoopHosts`, each stretch at the RAM the snapshot that starts it shows), across the last 10 minutes (`CLOUD_SERVER_INCOME_WINDOW_MS`). Nothing is bought until the snapshots span the window, and a disconnect starts it again. Money from work (crime, a company job) counts too, which errs toward buying.
- **The next purchase** (`chooseCloudServerPurchase`):
  - **Where:** under the game's limit of 25 (the owner's own cloud servers count toward it), a new server, `pserv-<n>`, the least n no server's name is or starts with followed by `-` (so a purchase the game renamed, `pserv-1-0`, makes the brain pass `pserv-1` over rather than buy it again and again); at the limit, the smallest of the brain's own (ties to the first name), upgraded. The owner's own cloud servers, under any other name, are never upgraded; with all 25 the owner's, the dashboard says there's no room.
  - **What it trusts:** a server's RAM must be a positive number to count, and a RAM the game sells (a power of two) to be upgraded from; hostnames must be strings. Snapshot data is the Agent's word.
  - **How much:** the largest power of two of RAM, at least 8 GB for a new server (three of the loop's 2.4 GB threads), whose price money covers, less the reserve, and whose **payback**, its price over the RAM it adds times the income per GB a second, is within `CLOUD_SERVER_PAYBACK_LIMIT_MS` (15 minutes). In BN1 a GB costs the same at every size, so the payback is too: the rule buys the largest money allows, or nothing.
  - **Kept in flight:** the purchase last sent is sent again while it's still to do and money and payback still allow it, so its id (and the Agent's count of tries) holds as money rises, and a purchase that keeps failing reaches `intentStuck`.
- **Order and reserve** (`DesiredStateController`): one purchase at a time. A program or home RAM upgrade the money covers goes first, and the dashboard says the cloud server waits for it. While the cheaper of the two waits for money, its price is held back from cloud servers if the hack loop's income would bring what's still short of it within `CLOUD_SERVER_RESERVE_HORIZON_MS` (1 minute): it's nearly there, so it's bought first. One further off isn't held back (SQLInject.exe's $250m, a large home RAM upgrade): cloud servers raise the income that brings it nearer, and once they stop paying back, money gathers for it anyway.
- **Sent as an intent** (#141): `buyServer { hostname, ram }` (#159), or `upgradeServer { hostname, ram }` (#194), after the programs' and home RAM's.
- **Fail loudly:**
  - A purchase not done after 5 tries is `intentStuck`, and fails the run report, as any intent.
  - A purchase the Agent reports done with a problem (bought under another name) is noted, raises `cloudServerProblem`, and stops the rule: the dashboard says it waits for you, and turning the switch off and on again goes on. The run report fails on it. The note is the brain's, in memory: a restarted brain buys again (the report still fails on the purchase that went wrong). A purchase not done, with a problem ("not enough money"), is tried again, and isn't noted.
- **The dashboard** shows the purchase being made, its RAM, price and payback, or what it waits for, and the cloud servers with their RAM.
- **The run report** gives the fleet a line a cycle (with `--verbose`, each cloud server bought or upgraded, with its RAM and price), and the hack loop's hosts' RAM, first snapshot → last in each BitNode ([the run report](#the-run-report)).

### The cloud server suite

`test/cloud-servers-income.test.ts` runs an 8 GB fresh start in BN1 with the brain's rules as on Steam but the batcher off (#206; the payback rule was tuned on the hack loop) and augmentations off (#222: the count rule installs within the 3 hours, which loses the cloud servers and the money compared), for 3 virtual hours, once with cloud servers switched off (as `main` was before #194) and once on. It checks that cloud servers were bought, each is a host of the loop's and runs it, none of their purchases is stuck or went wrong, the run with them earned more ("earned": money's rises added up, as #187's `earnedSince`), and its run report gives the fleet in the cycle (each purchase with `--verbose`) and the hosts' RAM, and passes. Over the seeds run, the runs with cloud servers must also have earned more than those without even less what the servers cost (one seed may not: those bought last haven't paid back by the end, so CI runs seeds 1 and 2, about 65 s). CI runs seeds 1 and 2; `CLOUD_SERVER_SEEDS=1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8` runs others, and each seed prints both runs' earnings, money at the end, the hosts' RAM, and the purchases.

**8 seeds, 3 virtual hours, at the tuned constants** (`CLOUD_SERVER_SEEDS=1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8`; off is `main`'s behaviour; measured with the faction state's slow-snapshot reading, #197, and the batch's code, #204, merged):

| Seed | Earned, off → on | Money at the end, off → on | Hosts' RAM at the end, off → on | First purchase |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | $4.69b → $15.84b | $3.00b → $1.72b | 2,972 → 207,772 GB | 90 min |
| 2 | $5.49b → $15.69b | $0.71b → $5.57b | 4,604 → 145,916 GB | 86 min |
| 3 | $4.46b → $24.04b | $2.78b → $1.36b | 3,188 → 195,700 GB | 89 min |
| 4 | $6.26b → $18.49b | $1.56b → $6.47b | 4,412 → 158,012 GB | 83 min |
| 5 | $6.67b → $21.20b | $1.85b → $8.51b | 3,668 → 179,796 GB | 81 min |
| 6 | $5.00b → $14.64b | $0.25b → $5.00b | 3,004 → 122,812 GB | 85 min |
| 7 | $4.89b → $9.36b | $3.19b → $2.23b | 2,860 → 74,540 GB | 90 min |
| 8 | $3.16b → $10.32b | $1.42b → $1.12b | 2,884 → 112,452 GB | 99 min |
| All 8 | $40.6b → $129.6b (×3.2) | $14.8b → $32.0b (×2.2) | | |

- **Every seed earned more,** 1.9 to 5.4 times as much. All 8 ended with 25 servers, and the servers cost $64.3b in all, $8.0b a run on average.
- **Less what the servers cost,** the 8 runs earned $65.3b, still above the $40.6b of the runs without. Seed 1 didn't on its own, by 2.5% ($4.57b against $4.69b): the servers bought last hadn't paid back by the end.
- **Nothing is bought for the first 80 to 100 minutes.** On n00dles a GB earns under the $61 a second a 15-minute payback needs. The first purchases come once the loop has moved to a richer target.
- **Other purchases** (in the tuning runs below): every program up to HTTPWorm.exe came when it did without cloud servers, home's 1 TB a minute later, and SQLInject.exe about 13 minutes later.
- **No stall:** no seed's money went 10 minutes without rising (each run's report passes).

**Tuning** (8 seeds, 3 hours; "less spent" is earned less what the servers cost, "last 30 min" the income over the run's last half hour, not measured in the first runs). The reserve was first on the whole price of the purchase waiting, then (#207's review) on what's still short of it, the rule now:

| Payback limit / reserve horizon | Earned | Less spent | Money at the end | Last 30 min |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| no cloud servers | $40.7b | $40.7b | $14.8b | $17.4b |
| **On what's short:** | | | | |
| **15 min / 1 min** | **$115.9b** | **$53.0b** | **$28.4b** | **$58.9b** |
| 15 / 2 | $114.2b | $43.2b | $18.9b | $60.2b |
| 15 / 5 (one seed went 11 min without a rise) | $107.5b | $53.7b | $28.2b | $60.0b |
| 15 / 15 | $91.3b | $41.3b | $15.0b | $50.0b |
| **On the whole price:** | | | | |
| 15 / 5 | $118.0b | $60.3b | $34.4b | $56.5b |
| 15 / 15 | $101.7b | $45.8b | $18.1b | $57.8b |
| 15 / 30 | $70.9b | $36.2b | $7.4b | $38.1b |
| 30 / 5 | $125.2b | $20.7b | $12.1b | $59.7b |
| 30 / 15 | $105.6b | $25.2b | $9.8b | $56.7b |
| 30 / 30 | $68.8b | $30.5b | $10.0b | |
| 30 / 60 | $52.1b | $31.0b | $8.4b | $27.4b |
| 60 / 30 | $75.9b | $24.3b | $0.3b | |
| 120 / 30 | $73.3b | $22.0b | $1.5b | $37.4b |
| 240 / 30 | $74.1b | $20.8b | $3.4b | $38.0b |

- **The payback limit:** a longer one buys more, but spends more than it brings back by the end: the income per GB is the hosts' average, and a GB more earns less than that on the same targets. At 15 minutes, purchases paid back within the run. Over 5 hours (seeds 1–4, the whole price, 30-minute horizon), 30 minutes earned $138.7b against $62.1b without, $71.8b less spending; 60 minutes $135.7b, $42.9b.
- **The reserve horizon:** a longer one holds money back for home RAM, which the hack loop doesn't run on, and the runs earn less. On what's short, a purchase is held back sooner than on its whole price (an expensive one nearly reached), so the horizon that does best is shorter: 1 minute, against 5 on the whole price.
- **A round's hacks capped at 90% of the target's money** was tried for seed 1's 11 minutes at 15 / 5: phantasy's $600m hacked to $0.6m in one round. It took the runs' earnings to barely above the runs without, and halved the [income suite](#the-income-suite)'s, so it was dropped; the stall didn't recur at 1 minute.

## Backdoors for the hacker factions

M4 phase A's third slice ([#195](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/195), epic #191): the brain asks for a backdoor on CSEC, avmnite-02h, I.I.I.I and run4theh111z as soon as each can be installed, so CyberSec, NiteSec, The Black Hand and BitRunners invite the player: each faction's only invitation requirement is its server's backdoor (`Faction/FactionInfo.tsx:489`, `:464-466`, `:419`, `:402`). Joining them is [slice A5's](#factions-and-their-work). The code is `src/backdoors.ts`; the four, with their factions, are core's `HACKER_FACTION_SERVERS`.

- **The rule** (`chooseBackdoor`), from the connected Agent's static snapshot (each server's `requiredHackingSkill`) and medium snapshot (`hasAdminRights`, `backdoorInstalled`, the hacking level): the first of the four, in that order, that's rooted and not backdoored, with the hacking level at or above its required level, as the terminal's `backdoor` checks (`Terminal/commands/backdoor.ts:23-32`). The order is the game's: each needs more ports (1 to 4) and a higher level (51–60, 202–220, 340–365, 505–550, rolled per game). One that can't go yet is passed over, so it never holds up a later one; what the snapshots don't say as expected (a server missing, no level) fails closed.
- **One at a time,** sent as the `backdoor` intent (#162) after the owner's queue and the purchase, so a `buy` never waits behind its terminal action. If the owner queued the same backdoor, it goes once, as the owner's. The intents list shows its source as `backdoor`.
- **Asked for until the connected Agent reports it done,** not only until a medium snapshot shows it installed: a backdoor longer than the Agent's 2 s round goes on alone, and the try that finds it installed sends the terminal `home` (decision 42). Done, the Agent never runs it again while it's sent, so it's never sent twice; after a disconnect, the next Agent's worker finds it installed and only sends the terminal home.
- **How long:** a quarter of the server's hacking time (`Terminal/Terminal.ts:233-243`), `5 × (2.5 × required × security + 500) / (hacking + 50)` s over the speed multipliers (`Hacking.ts:60-80`). These four start at security 1: their data's 0 is skipped as falsy (`Server/ServerHelpers.ts:367`), so the server's default applies (`Server/Server.ts:79-83`). That's about 8 s for CSEC at hacking 55, and 4 to 5 s for the others at their levels; about 3.3 times that in BN14 (`HackingSpeedMultiplier` 0.3).
- **Known limit:** a brain restarted between a backdoor's end and the Agent's try that sends the terminal home leaves the terminal on that server (decision 42): no snapshot says which server the terminal is on, so the new brain can't tell.
- **No switch:** a backdoor costs no money and changes nothing a later choice depends on (#162). The hack loop roots the servers (#134); with it off, a server nothing else roots waits for root.
- **After an install** the game recreates the servers (`Prestige.ts:74`, `:98`), so the four are backdoored again.
- **The brain's log:** "backdoor: asking for CSEC (hacking 55, needs 55), for CyberSec's invitation", then "backdoor: CSEC backdoored, 10 s after it was asked for" (between medium snapshots). "backdoor REPEATED: asking for CSEC again, though the game showed it backdoored at …, since its last reset" if a server it saw backdoored in this game (the same `lastAugReset`) shows not backdoored again: only a save loaded over the game, or a bug, would do that. Not before the connected Agent's slow snapshot says the reset, since after an install the next Agent's medium snapshot can come first. Another `lastAugReset` also forgets the backdoor asked for (refused at the BitVerse, say, then the destroy). A snapshot that doesn't show the server isn't taken for a reset.
- **The run report** lists each server's backdoor, and the invitation that followed (a `gameDialog` event of kind `factionInvitation` naming the faction), and fails on a backdoor overdue or asked for again ([the run report](#the-run-report)). A backdoor that fails is an intent not done, tried every 30 s: after 5 tries, `intentStuck`. One due for 5 minutes, asked for or not, is the dashboard's `backdoorOverdue` alert: the controller notes, for each server, the first medium snapshot in a row that showed it due (`backdoorsDue()`).

### The backdoor suite

`test/backdoors-simulation.test.ts`: an 8 GB fresh start in BN1, the brain as on Steam (the hack loop, the programs and home RAM on). The brain buys BruteSSH.exe, the hack loop roots CSEC, and once the hacking level reaches CSEC's the brain asks for its backdoor once; the simulator's `sim` effector types it, and the brain logs it backdoored and stops asking. The save then holds CyberSec joined (the invitation, joined by the brain since #203), the terminal is home, and the run report lists the backdoor (its intent through `sim` with `--verbose`) and passes. The simulator has no page, so no invitation dialog: the report says "no invitation from CyberSec seen". CI runs seed 1 (about 4 s); `BACKDOOR_SEEDS=1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8` runs others. On seeds 1 to 8, CSEC (needing 52 to 60) was asked for at the medium snapshot where the level reached it, and backdoored between 12.9 and 14.9 virtual minutes.

## Factions and their work

M4 phase A's fifth slice ([#203](https://github.com/UnderMyBed/bitburner-agent/issues/203), epic #191): the brain joins the factions that invite the player and works for the faction and work type that reach the augmentation with the most hacking value per second of work, so a run earns the reputation A6 buys with. Every figure is the game's (owner decision 46). The code is `src/factions.ts`; the formulas are core's ([faction work](../core/README.md#faction-work)), and so, since #260, is the choice itself (`factionWorkChoice`; with A6's `donationTermsOf` and `countablesToCome` and the city group's `cityGroupValues` and `chooseCityGroup`, [core: the brain's choices, shared with the fast model](../core/README.md#the-brains-choices-shared-with-the-fast-model)), so the fast model ([packages/model](../model/README.md)) makes the same one.

- **Joining** (`chooseJoin`): every invitation in the slow snapshot's faction state (#197) from a faction with no enemies, one at a time, in the order invited, as the `joinFaction` intent (#147) after the purchase and the backdoor; asked for until a medium snapshot (`player.factions`) or the slow one shows it joined. One whose invitation is gone, joined or not (an install), is dropped. **No switch:** joining such a faction costs nothing and bans nothing (`Faction/FactionHelpers.tsx:35-52`). If the owner queued the same join, it goes once, as the owner's. The intents list shows its source as `join`.
- **The city factions: the brain chooses** (#228, [owner decision 54](../../docs/decisions/owner-decisions.md), [invitations](#invitations-and-daedaluss-count)): it joins the factions of the city group it chose this cycle that sell something of value, and never another group's; their invitations are passed over, on the dashboard ("Sector-12's invitation not joined: another city group's (it would ban …)") and in the run report, and logged once. Each such join is irreversible (it bans the other groups' factions), so since #269 it goes through [the irreversible gate](#intents) like the owner's: its guard on two medium snapshots, a save backup, one irreversible intent at a time, and after an install or a destroy queued before it. Until then the dashboard says why it isn't sent ("not sent: waiting for a save backup"). While it waits there, the first other invitation from a faction without enemies is joined beside it, so a long wait (backups failing, say) holds up no join that bans nothing; the factions panel says so ("Joining: Sector-12; CyberSec beside it, a join with enemies"), and the log as it starts and ends.
- **Working** (`chooseFactionWork`), from the slow snapshot's faction state and reset info, and the medium snapshot's player:
  - **Worth having:** an augmentation's hacking value is core's `hackingValue` at the player's hacking level: the logarithms of its speed, money, growth, chance, experience and faction reputation multipliers added up, and its hacking skill multiplier as the experience multiplier that lifts the level as much at that level (`hackingSkillValue`), so a skill augmentation counts for more as the level climbs.
